Introduction

The National Survey of Family Growth, Cycle I was conducted in 1973
by the National Opinion Research Center under contract to the National
Center for Health Statistics. Home interviews were conducted with 9,797
women 15-44 years of age who were married, previously married, or sizgle
with children of their owm in the household. The data have been used by
the National Center for Health Statistics as the basis for a series of
reports on the determinants and consequences of patterns of fanily
formation and fertility in the United States. To make the data available
to a larger number of persons, a standardized micro-data tape transcripc
has been prepared for distribution. This Tape Documentation Manual
describes that tape transcript; other items needed to use the tape
transcript include the reel of magnetic tape containing the transcript
itself, a report on the Origin and History of the National Survey of
Family Growth, a report on the Semple Design, Estimarion Procedures,
Variance Estimation for Cycle I of the National Survey of Family Growth,
and the interview schedule. The complete package of tape and docu-
mentation may be obtained from the National Center for Health Statistics.

Omitted Dara Items

Some items of data collectad by NSFG, Cvcle-I are not included in this
file. The items deleced end the reascns for deletion are discussed
below by major type.

(1) Unreliability. Because of an ambiguity in the instructions to
interviewers about the saquence of questions, Question 1293 was not
asked of many women. The resulting high level of missing data makes the
reliability of the information doubtful, and for that reason it is not
included in this file.

(2) Experimental methodology. In Question 190, a randomized
response technique was used. In this technique the respondent answers
one of two questions selected by her at random, but with a known
probability. Knowing that probability and the distribution of responses
to one of the two questions (from outside information), it is possible
to infer the distribution of responses to the other question. The
technique allows the respondent to answer a question about a sensitive
matter (in this case, abortion) without revealing the answer to the
interviewer, and may, therefore, increase the validity of the response.
Because the technique is relatively new and untested, however, its use
in NSFG, Cycle-I was regarded as experimental, and it is not included in

this file.

(3) Interview characteristics. Questions A.-F. on page 47 of the
interview schedule were completed by the interviewer, and concern
characteristics of the interview rather than the respondent herseli.
Their purpose was quality control rather than analysis, and technical
reports on these and related data will be prepared on an occasional
basis.

Missing Data

When a data item was missing for a respondent, whether because she
tefused to answer, didn't know the answer, or was not asked the question,
it was usually imputed by a "hot deck” procedure. If the proportion of
eligible respondents for whom the data item was missing was relatively
low (less than 2%), a simple imputation procedure was used: The records
of eligible respondents were ordered according to the categories creatad
by cross-classifying age, race, and marital status; each record was then
read; if it had an item of data missing, it was imputed, using the value
of that item in the preceding record. Because the preceding record was
like the record with missing data in respect to age, race, and marizcal
Status, the imputed values for missing data cases would be distributed
like the known cases in each catagory. The procedure for items where

the proportion of missing data was larger (2% or more) was somewhat mora
complex, although the same “hot deck" philosophy was followed: for eact
such item, variables were selected. which were believed to be closely
related to thea imputed item; using a cross-classification of those
variables, a matrix was created inside the computer; as each record was
read, if it was not missing daca in the pertinent item, it was assigned
to its appropriate cell of the matrix, displacing the previous occupant
of that call; if a record was missing the pertinent data itam, it was
imputed to be the same as the value for that item of the case occupying
the matrix position to which the missing data case would heve been
assigned. Following each set of imputations, a series of checks wer
made to insure that the imputed values were consistent with other it
on the record; when an inconsistency was discovered (e.g., a second
marriage which began before a first marriage), the case was individually
considered, and values were imputed which made the data items consistenc
with one another.

e=s

In a few cases missing data were not imputed, either because the
proportion of missing data was very high (15% or more) or vecause there
was no clear set of variables to use as a basis for imputation. - -

which missing data were impuced, however, the number of cs z

Semple Design, Estimation Procedures, and Variance Estimation

A complete description of the sampling design, estimation procedures
and variance estimation was included as a separate item in the Tape
Transcript package. Those matters are discussed here only as they
pertain to the tape documentation manual.

sou

All analyses using these data should be "weighted;" that is, each
sample case should be counted as if it were the number of women in the
population it was designed to represent. That number, the "weight" for
each case, is shown in positions 734-739.

Since the weighted statistics computed from these data are basad on
a sample, they will differ somewhat from the figures that would have
been obtained if a complete census hac been taken using the same schedules.
instructions, and interviewing personnel and procedures. The variacions

. +

that might occ

r by chance because only a sample of the population is

surveyed is measured by a statistic called the "standard error."
Standard errors of statistics from complex samples, such as that in the
NSFG, are computed by NCHS using the "balanced half-sample replicazion

method." That technicue is described in the following oublications:

Sealeh Sratiscics. PHS Pub. No. 1000-Series 2?-No. 14. Dlublic

Nacional Cenzerz far Eealrh Statisrices: Distribution and
properties of variance estimators for compl Ltistage probabilicy

samples: an empirical distribution. Vital and Health Statistics.

DHEW Pub. No. (HRA) 75-1339. Public Health Service. Washington.

U.S. Government Printing Office, March 1975.

Standard errors for some common statistics have been computed for
the NSFG, and may be found in the "Technical Notes" to NCHS publications
based on the survey. The table below shows Standatd errors for selectad
total numbers and percentages. The chances are ab! 68 out of 100 thac
an estimate from the sample would differ from a complete census by less
than the standard error. The changes are about 95 out of 100 that the
difference would be less than twicé the standard error and about 99 out
of 100 that it would be less than 24 times as large.

Standard errors for statistics not found here nor in NCHS publications
may be computed by the balanced half-sample replication method. The

method requires creating many sub-samples each containing one-half of

the casas selected according to rules described in the pudlications

cited above. The information necessary to create the hali-samplag i

uh

found in positions 748-749 ("pseudostratum") and 750 (“pair number”).

Some computer soft-ware sackages for statistical analysis include prozgvans

t

for computing standard arrors using the balanced half-sampole raplicacion

Date Codas

During the interview, dates were obtained either as the year elone, as
month and year, or as day. month, and year. For this file, year and
month were transformed into "century months" by muliciplying the year by

+

12 and adding the number of the month, where January = 1, February = 2,
and so on. For instance, the cencury month code for February 1959 is
(59 x 12) + 2 = 710. The cencur:’ mrenth form is more convenient for
computing an interval between dazes.

The century month codes from 301 through 900 are shown in the array
below, with the years from 1925 through 1974 on the vertical axis and
the months on the horizontai axis. The code for a given month and year
can be found by reading across the line for the appropriate year to the
column headed by the appropriate month.

In cases where the month was noc known, the first digit of the date code
was set equal to 9, and the last two digits were se’ qual to the
reported year. For instance, if an event occurred an unknown month
during 1968, the date code would be 968, the 9 indicating the month is
unknown, the 68 indicating the known year of the event.

In cases where the year of the event was not known, refused or not
ascertained, the date code was 999.

In cases where the question asked about the date use of contraception
was temminaced, bur che raessendan= was still using @ contraceptive, the
qeestion was inarglicaslz, and the dare code was sec equal to 997.

In cases where the question was inapplicable for any other reason, the
position is BLANK.

There are two items in which exceptions to these data code rules were
made, those in positions 55-57 and 60-62. Both are based on questions
about future events, and responses included some dates after 1974,
which, when coded according to the century mench formula given above, --
result in values over $00; since values over 900 are normally used for
the unknown and not applicable codes, there is a potential of ambiguous
codes in these positions. Eowever, there is an unambiguous code for

each case in these posisrions, and ict is described in the code dascrincion
of each.

OCCUPATIONAL CLASSIFICATION

This is a 5-digit occupational classification; the first 3 digits are
the 1970 U.S. Census occupational codes, and the last 2 digits are the
1960 Hodge~Siegel Prestige Score.** This score was originally designed
for use with the 1960 U.S. Census occupational codes and has been
adapted to the 1970 list by Frances D. Harris, Coding Supervisor. The
adaptations were made as follows:

1)

2)

3)

4)

RK

If the occupation was a new one assigned to a previously existin
sub-division (e.g. Engineers within the major category 'Professiona?,
Technical, and Kindred Workers') it received the prestige score for
the general n.a.c. category of that sub-division.

If the occupation was grouped with a like occupation in 1960 and
then divided in 1970--both occupations received the same 1960
prestige score.

If this occupation was completely new to the list and was not
assigned to an old sub-division, it received the prestige score cf
the n.e.c. category for the major occupational division.

All adaptations are marked with a "+" next to the occupational
classifications. The original Prestige Score has a one~place
decimal fraction. All decimal fractions .5 and over were rounded
up to the next highest whole number.

The craft score was used for apprentices when the craft score was
less than 41. If the craft score was greater than 41, then the
apprentice score of 41 was used. 1

According to the present U.S. Census definition, current members of
the armed forces are not included in the civilian population labor
force. However, because they do fall inco our samples, a code has

been provided ror them. *

Organization of the Data

The transcript contains 9797 records. Each record has 2100 positions
containing information from one sample woman. As shown in the accompanying
diagram, the information on each record is in two parts: first, information
about the woman herself in positions 1-787, followed by information
about her pregnancy intervals in positions 940-2023. Some blank positions
follow each of the two parts (788-839 and 2024-2100). The information
about the woman is arranged on the tape as it appeared in the interview
schedule, except that a few items have bean omitted and a few items
added at the end. The arrangement is generally by broad topical areas,
but to improve the flow of the interview, items on similar topics were
sometimes separated. Note also that some questions were skipped for
some women, dapending on their answers to aarlier questions, and that
slightly dicferenc fornus of the interview schedule were used for currently
married and previously married women; the user should examine che
sequence of questions in which the item of intarest occurs to determine
which sample women were asked the question, and in which form the
question was asked. Following the information about the woman taken. ~

irectly from the incerview schedule are a number of "recodes"” (positions
712-787); they are variables created during data analysis by combining
interview items.

The second part of aach racordé contains information about intervals ~
between pregnancies, arranged by type of interval: open interval
(between the end of the last pregnancy and the dace of interview),

tactervals afrer July 1, 19790, intervals ending in a live birth, and all
pregnancy intervals. s one open interval, except

ae

currently;

we

the amount of space allowed on the
record for each type of interval is the same for all women, and is equal
to the amount of space needed to record all the information for the
woman with the largest number of intervals of the type. This means, of
course, that if a woman had fewer than the maximum number of intervals,
there are some blank positions in her record for thet type of interval.
Information which was asked about all intervals is in the section for
all intervals; information in other sections was asked only about
intervals of that particular type. Information ebout the same interval
may appear in different sections; for instance, an interval after

July 1, 1970 which endad in 2 Live birth should have information in both
the section on intervals after July 1, 1970, the section on

intervals ending in live birth, and the section on all intervals.
Information from two sections may be combined by matching the interval

order number which is shown near the beginning c£ each section.

The derinition of wantecness is based on direct responses to a number of questions
about each pregnancy 2a woman has conceived. For a woman wno reports that contraceptive
use was stoppe¢c prior to conception, and for those who report that no contraceptive
method was used in the interval preceding conception (which begins with the end of the
preceding pregnancy, if there is one), the question on wantedness is phrased as follows:
"Was the reason you (were not/stopped) using any method because you, yourself, wanted

to become pregnant?" An affirmative response to this question is defined as a "wanted"
pregnancy; if the woman answers negatively, she is asked two follow-up questions which
are identical to those asked of all other respondents. These questions are: ‘At *

time you became pregnant (THIS INTERVAL) did you, yourself, actually want to have z whe
baby at some time?" and "ds you recall, is that how you felt before you became pregnant,
or did you come to feel that way later?" The latter question is rephrased as follows for

» 227
t an who indicated that they did not know or care whether or not chey wanted to have
a(nother) baby: "It is sometimes difficult to recall these things, but as you look back
to just before that pregnancy began, would you say you probably wanted a(nother) baby
some time or probably not?" ;

A pregnancy is defined as "wanted" if the woman reports that (a) contraception was
not used in the interval or was stopped prior to conceptior because the woman wanted to
become pregnant; or (b) she wanted to have a(nother) baby ac some time and felt that way
before becoming pregnant; or (c) she reported that she probably wanted a(nother) baby at
some time. A pregnancy is defined as "unwanted" if the woman reports that she did not
want, or probably did noc want to have a(nother) baby at some time and felt that way
before becoming pregnant. All other pregnancies are termed "undetermined." The table
below shows the breakdown by the sub-categories of the components of wantedness.

Percentage distribution of sub-categories of wantedness b race
for all pregnancies regardless of outcome: United Staces, 1973.

* The number of children a woman expects to give birth to in the future, including the
child she is carrying if she is currently pregnant. Women who were sterile or married to
sterile men were classified as expecting zero adidtional births. Those physically able

to have births in the future were asked whether they and their husbands intended to have
any babies in the future and, if so, how many. Women who did not know whether they
intended any future births, or did not know a particular number they intended to have, =
were asked for the smallest and largest numbers they would expect to have. Women who
reported a particular number of children they intended to have were asked how sure they
were about having just that number. Those who were not sure of having just that number
were asked for the maximum and minimum numbers they would expect to have.

For each woman, there is a maximum, minimum and central number of addirional births
expected. £ a woman reported a specific number she intended to have, that was considerec
the central number she expected (unless it was above her maximum or below her minimum
expected number) and, if she was very sure about it, that was also the minimum and maximu:-.
number she expected. For a woman who was not sure of having just her intended number, the
smallest and largest numbers she expected were her minimum and maximum numbers, respective
For a woman who did not report any specific number of intended future births, the average
of the smallest and the largest numbers expected became her central expected number. In
this report, discussion of additional births expected refers to the central number of
additional births expected, unless specifically identified in some other way.

* The classification by contraceptive status is derived from several topics covered in the
questionnaire including Pregnancy status, fecundity, current use or non-use of contraceptio:
and specific contraceptive methods being used. A first broad division is made between
noncontraceptors and contraceptors, each of which is further subclassified.

Noncontraceptive status is classified as:
Pregnant - A woman (or couple) was classed as pregnant if she replied affirmatively to
the question “Are you pregnant now?" or for those in doubt, "Do you think you probabl
are pregnant or not?'' A woman who reported thac the onset of her lasc menstrual perio
was within the last 30 days prior to the interview was automatically considered not
pregnant.

Seeking pregnancy - A woman (or couple) was classified as seeking pregnancy if she
~eported she was not using a method at the time of interview because she wanted to
ome pregnant.

° 238

Postpartum - A woman (or couple) was classified as postpartum if she reported she was
not currently using a method, was not seeking a pregnancy, and her last pregnancy had
terminated within 2 months before the date she was interviewed.

Sterile ~ A woman (or couple) was classified as noncontraceptively sterile if she
reported that it was impossible for her to have another baby for any of the following
reasons: menopause, sterility due to accident, illness, or congenital causes, or

a sterilizing operation performed on the wife or the husband entirely for reasons
other than preventing future children. (Note that this definition differs from

that in the fecundity status recode, 767-768).

Other nonusers - Women (or couples) who reported thay were currently using no
contraceptive method and could not be classified in any of the preceding categories
of noncontraceptors were classified her. Among these are women who were indifferent
to the chances of pregnancy, had a very low risk of pregnancy due to some fecundity
impairment, or objected to contraceptive methods for personal or religious reasons.
Women who used the douche following intercourse, but who did not report this as a
method of contraception, were also classified here although such douching practice
is known to have a very modest contraceptive effect when done very soon after
intercourse.

Contraceptive status is classified as: \

Sterile - A woman (or couple) was classified as contraceptively sterile if she or
her husband had had a sterilizing operation that was done "at least partly so that
(they) would not have any more children." Surgical sterilizations for contraceptive
reasons are further classified according to female and male operations.

Method users - A woman (or couple) who tTeported use of a contraceptive method at the
date of interview was classified according to the specific method used. Methods used
by extremely small proportions of the population such as jelly, cream suppositories,
or abstinence, not in combination with any other methods, were grouped in the
category "Other." Where more than one method was reported in current use, the method =
generally considered the most effective was used for classification purposes. _- The
contraceptive pill, the intrauterine device (IUD), and contraceptive sterilization
were developed, or achieved prominence, since 1960 and are referred to as the modern
methods, while those prominent before 1960 are referred to as traditional methods.
Although women who douche for cleanliness only are listed here as "using
contraception", for many purposes they would be considered nonusers of

contraception, and will be so considered in NCHS publications.

* A respondent will be classified as sterile if one of the following is true:

a.

She or her husband are completely sterile due to operation, accident, illness
or other reason.

She reports she is menopausal and the date of her last normal menstrual perio

was six months or more before dace of interview.

(Note thar chis deZinicion differs from that in the current method use recode,

765-766).
A respondent will be classified as subfecund but not sterile if one of the following
is true:

a. She reports she is menopausal and the date of her last normal menstrual period

was less than six months before the date of interview.

There was surgical removal of part of the female reproductive system. -

There is a difficulty in getting pregnant or carrying the pregnancy to full
term, or danger to either the respondent, or the baby.

A medical doctor has advised the respondent never to become pregnant again
because of danger to her or the baby or doth, and the woman or her husband will
have a sterilizing operation or an abortion if she becomes pregnant again.

A currently married women has used no contraception in the most recent three
years of a marital union and has not become pregnant.

All other respondents will be classified as fecund.
